| PERCEPT-01 | Can a hitter answer a breaking ball differently from a fastball only when he can tell them apart early? MATCHUP-05F found hitter-specific family parts at the decision moment. If the cue is the release, those parts should matter more against pitchers whose breaking balls leave from a different point than their fastballs and less against pitchers who hide them. Each pitcher's separation between his fastballs (groups 0-2) and breaking balls (3-4) at release (x0 and z0 at 50 ft, and extension), in units of his own within-family scatter (training pitches 2023-2024, at least 60 of each); MATCHUP-05's models (league with its family part, development, 2025 scored); the family parts' gain over location maps by thirds of the pitcher's separation, and the difference between the separated and tight thirds, paired by game. Checked on synthetic seasons first: with family answers planted only when releases separate, separated minus tight +1.81 nats per 1,000 [1.46, 2.18]; with answers regardless of release, -0.38 [-0.76, +0.04] | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Prediction: the family parts' gain against the separated third exceeds the tight third by at least 0.5 nats per 1,000 decisions with the interval excluding zero. Decision rule: if it holds, release concealment (the separation in units of the pitcher's own scatter) becomes a pitcher scouting measure and a candidate scale on the family parts, scored once on the untouched months (PERCEPT-01F); if not, hitters' family answers do not depend on release cues at this resolution (they read the type from the flight before the decision moment). Results (run 37897866419; 801 pitchers measured, thirds cut at 0.98 and 1.50 scatter units): the family parts gain +2.66 [2.29, 3.06] against tight releases, +2.40 [2.04, 2.75] in the middle third, +2.72 [2.36, 3.11] against separated releases; **separated minus tight +0.06 [-0.52, +0.65]**; on breaking balls outside +3.49, +4.11, +4.22; the league-wide family pattern +5.06, +4.72, +4.98. **The prediction failed: hiding the breaking ball's release does not blunt a hitter's family-specific answer at the decision moment.** Reading: by 260 ms before the plate the type is readable from the flight whatever the release; PERCEPT-02 asks the same question of the flight itself (the two families' separation where they appear headed at the decision moment). | failed: no release effect (+0.06 [-0.52, +0.65]) |
| PERCEPT-02 | PERCEPT-01 with the plainest type cue in flight instead of the release: each pitcher's speed gap between his fastballs and breaking balls, in units of his own within-family speed scatter (a hard slider a few mph under the fastball against a curveball 15 mph under). Same models and split as PERCEPT-01 (development, 2025). No new synthetic check (PERCEPT-01's split with a different separation measure) | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Prediction: the family parts' gain against the third with the largest speed gaps exceeds the third with the smallest by at least 0.5 nats per 1,000 decisions, interval excluding zero. Decision rule: if it holds, speed-gap concealment becomes a pitcher scouting measure and a candidate scale on the family parts (PERCEPT-02F on the untouched months); if not, neither release nor speed gap governs whether hitters answer types differently. Results (run 37898229237; thirds cut at 4.7 and 6.8 scatter units): family parts +2.38 [2.03, 2.72] against the smallest speed gaps, +2.55 [2.16, 2.91] middle, +2.95 [2.48, 3.37] largest; **largest minus smallest +0.58 [0.03, 1.14]**; on breaking balls outside +3.01, +4.57, +4.58; the league-wide family pattern flat (+4.98, +4.86, +4.89). **The prediction held, barely.** Reading, with its limit: hitters' own answers to breaking balls are clearer against pitchers whose breaking balls are much slower than their fastballs, as if speed were the cue; but the speed gap also separates curveballs from hard sliders, so the pitch kind may carry it rather than perception. Under the rule, PERCEPT-02F scores it once on the untouched months, with a sliders-only split reported beside it. | passed (dev), barely: +0.58 [0.03, 1.14] |
| PERCEPT-02F | PERCEPT-02 scored once on the untouched months (another look at them; this candidate's only one): models fitted on 2023-2025 at MATCHUP-05's shrinkage (10 and 10), speed gaps from 2023-2025 pitches, every 2026 regular-season pitch from August 1 to September 27. Two scorings from the same frozen code: (a) the registered measure (fastballs against breaking balls, groups 3-4), which decides; (b) sliders and sweepers only (group 3), reported to see whether the pitch kind rather than the speed cue carries it | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Prediction for (a): the family parts' gain against the largest-gap third exceeds the smallest-gap third with the interval excluding zero (development +0.58 [0.03, 1.14]; the untouched months hold about a third as many decisions, so the interval will be wider). Decision rule: if it holds, speed-gap concealment is credited as a pitcher scouting measure (how much a pitcher's breaking ball blunts hitters' type-specific answers); if not, it is not confirmed and stays a development result. Results (runs 37898561350 and 37898583663, frozen f7dba16; 226,679 untouched decisions; 965 pitchers measured): (a) family parts +2.21 [1.66, 2.80] against the smallest speed gaps, +2.81 middle, +1.64 [0.85, 2.38] largest; **largest minus smallest -0.58 [-1.49, +0.33]**; (b) sliders and sweepers only -0.20 [-1.15, +0.83]. **The prediction failed: the development difference did not hold up (it reversed sign).** With PERCEPT-01, neither the release nor the speed gap governs whether hitters answer a breaking ball differently from a fastball at the decision moment. | failed: -0.58 [-1.49, +0.33] |
| DRIFT-01 | What changes before strikeouts and walks do. Each hitter's swing map at the decision moment refitted on each 30-day window of a season (ridge, offset by a league map fitted on 2023), read on one fixed set of 4,000 pitches outside the zone: a chase propensity free of what he was thrown. Its change in the latest window against his earlier windows of the season, next to the raw chase rate's change, as predictors of strikeouts and walks in the following window beyond his rates in the latest window and earlier in the season (feed 2023-2025; logistic models trained on 2023-2024 plate appearances, scored on 2025, paired by game). Checked on synthetic seasons first: with a planted drift in chase propensity both changes predict next-window strikeouts (about 1 nat per 1,000 plate appearances); with none, 0.00 | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions on 2025: the map-based change predicts next-window strikeouts beyond the rates (gain at least 0.2 nats per 1,000 plate appearances, interval excluding zero; coefficient positive) and walks (coefficient negative); it adds to the raw chase rate's change (interval excluding zero), since the raw rate moves with the pitches a hitter happens to see. Decision rule: if it holds, a hitter's decision-moment chase propensity, kept current by window, becomes an input to the player model's next refit and a scouting signal (whose approach is changing before his line shows it); if it adds nothing beyond the raw chase rate, the decision-moment map is a matchup tool, not an early-warning one. Results (run 37878336284; 270,377 plate appearances in 2025, 6,066 window maps): the map-based chase change has SD 1.9 points against 5.1 for the raw chase rate's change, correlation 0.65. Strikeouts: map change gain -0.002 nats per 1,000 plate appearances [-0.037, +0.034], coefficient -0.007 per point (the wrong sign); the raw change +0.024 [-0.005, +0.053]; map over raw -0.019 [-0.041, +0.003]. Walks: map change coefficient -0.012 per point (the predicted sign) but gain -0.001 [-0.045, +0.039]; map over raw +0.018 [-0.012, +0.048]. **Every prediction failed.** Under the rule the decision-moment map is a matchup tool, not an early-warning one: a month-to-month change in how a hitter chases, measured free of what he was thrown, does not tell where his strikeouts and walks go next beyond his recent rates. What changes first, if anything, has to be looked for in the swing itself (bat speed, timing) rather than in swing decisions. | failed; maps are not an early warning |
| STEER-01 | Where the bat aims, read from misses. MATCHUP-03 found misses follow the true crossing far better than the decision-moment projection. If the bat is steered until a later moment and no further (the Decision Horizon's second clock, 100 to 125 ms, estimated from launch angles under an assumed bat-ball geometry), misses should be best explained by the flight as it looked then. Whiffs on 2023-2025 swings (feed), one location map shared by all pitch types (as in the horizon's first run) on the gravity-only projection from tau seconds out, with pitch family, speed, two strikes, the hitter's whiff habit and platoon; fitted on 2023-2024, held-out log loss on 2025 for tau from 0 (the true crossing) to 0.26 s; bootstrap over games for the best tau. Checked on synthetic swings first: planted at 0.11 s the profile peaks at 0.11; planted at the true crossing it peaks at 0.03 and is flat to 0.05 | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: the profile peaks between 0.07 and 0.15 s, its gain over the true crossing positive with the interval excluding zero, and at least 80% of bootstrap draws pick a tau in that range. Falsifiers: the best tau at 0.03 or below (the bat follows the whole flight) or at 0.18 or above (contact is set at the decision). Decision rule: if it holds, the steering limit is confirmed from misses, independently of the launch-angle estimate, and the matchup engine's miss model reads location at that moment; if the bat follows the whole flight, the engine's miss model uses the true crossing. Results (run 37879657819; 336,383 swings of 2025): whiff log loss rises monotonically as the projection moves back from the plate: against the true crossing -0.09 nats per 1,000 swings at 0.03 s, -0.27 at 0.05, -0.61 at 0.07, -1.13 at 0.09, -1.96 at 0.11, -3.20 at 0.13, -5.0 at 0.15, -9.3 at 0.18, -18.5 at 0.22, -31.6 at 0.26; the true crossing is best in every bootstrap draw. **The falsifier triggered (best tau at 0.03 or below): read as one location map, misses follow where the ball actually arrives.** Under the rule the engine's miss model uses the true crossing (as it already does). What it does not settle: a single map cannot separate where the bat aims from where the ball ends; the steering idea predicts that the movement after the steering limit (unseen by the hitter) adds to the true crossing, which is a different model (true crossing plus the late movement after tau, profiled over tau) and needs its own registration. | falsifier triggered: misses follow the true crossing |
| MATCHUP-02 | Do decision-moment matchups move plate-appearance outcomes? Hitter maps and the league map at the decision moment as in MATCHUP-01 (2023-2024, shrinkage 10). For each 2025 hitter-pitcher pair, the pitcher's 2024 pitches (to the hitter's side when there are enough) are run through both: the pair's expected extra swing rate outside the zone (chase deviation) and inside it (zone-swing deviation), beyond the additive terms, all from data before 2025. Strikeouts and walks of every 2025 plate appearance on both players' earlier strikeout or walk rates (shrunk) and platoon, with and without the two deviations; game-parity cross-fitting for out-of-sample log loss, game-bootstrap intervals for the coefficients. Checked on synthetic data first: with planted hitter-specific zones the strikeout coefficient is +0.013 per point [0.007, 0.021] and the gain +0.24 nats per 1,000 plate appearances; with none, 0.005 [-0.012, 0.025] and 0.00 | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions on 2025: strikeouts rise with the chase deviation (+0.005 to +0.03 log-odds per point, interval excluding zero) and gain at least 0.1 nats per 1,000 plate appearances out of sample with the interval excluding zero; walks fall with it (interval excluding zero); the strikeout rate across chase-deviation quintiles rises beyond the base model, top against bottom by at least 0.5 points. Decision rule: if the strikeout prediction holds, a matchup layer is built for the simulator (each lineup hitter against each pitcher he may face, strikeout and walk chances moved by the fitted coefficients, from maps and arsenals of earlier seasons) and replayed with paired streams like any other change. Results (run 37877030474): 119,264 of 182,484 plate appearances in 2025 have pair features (55,084 pairs); chase deviation SD 2.6 points, zone-swing deviation 3.0. **Strikeouts: no effect** (chase coefficient -0.0037 per point [-0.0096, +0.0008], the wrong sign; gain -0.03 nats per 1,000 plate appearances [-0.07, +0.01]; across chase quintiles the observed rate, 22.6 to 19.6%, follows the base model, 23.1 to 20.3%). **Walks fall with the chase deviation**: -0.0176 per point [-0.0284, -0.0093], zone-swing deviation +0.0084 [0.0023, 0.0154], gain +0.07 [0.00, 0.14]; the top chase quintile walks 7.1% against 7.7% expected, the bottom 10.2% against 9.8%. Predictions failed for strikeouts and held for walks. **No matchup layer under the rule** (it was keyed on strikeouts). Reading: the pair-specific chasing MATCHUP-01 predicts is real and shows up where chasing acts directly (fewer balls, fewer walks); its net effect on strikeouts cancels (chased pitches end in weak contact as often as in misses, and plate appearances get shorter). Swing maps alone are too small a lever at the plate-appearance level; misses (MATCHUP-03) and contact are where a strikeout matchup effect would have to come from. | walks -0.0176/pt; no K effect; no layer |
| ENGINE-01 | The matchup engine played pitch by pitch. For each 2025 hitter-pitcher pair (to the hitter's side), the pitcher's 2024 pitches in each count are run through the hitter's swing map at the decision moment (MATCHUP-01) and his whiff map on the true crossing (MATCHUP-03), with league models for called strikes on takes and fouls on contact (all fitted on 2023-2024); a count-by-count chain from 0-0 gives the pair's strikeout and walk chances with his maps and with the league maps. The difference is split over the 2025 plate appearances into a hitter part, a pitcher part and the pair part (what the two averages hide), and the pair part into what the chain makes of his tendencies as constants and what the shape of his maps adds against this arsenal. Outcomes of every 2025 plate appearance on both players' earlier rates and platoon, then plus the hitter and pitcher parts, then plus the pair part; game-parity cross-fitting for out-of-sample log loss, game bootstrap for coefficients. Checked on synthetic games played pitch by pitch first: the chain's league strikeout chance matches the observed rate (42.63% against 42.64%); with planted hitter weaknesses up in the zone and pitchers who live high or low, the strikeout pair part gains 0.39 nats per 1,000 plate appearances [0.21, 0.58] and its shape part has coefficient 0.060 per point [0.048, 0.078]; with hitters differing only in level, the shape part is 0.037 [-0.036, 0.102] with no gain, while the undivided deviation showed a false 0.019 [0.001, 0.039] (it carries level information the earlier rates miss), which is why the split is the test | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions on 2025: (1) the strikeout pair part's coefficient positive with the interval excluding zero, and its shape part likewise; (2) the pair part's out-of-sample gain over the levels model positive with the interval excluding zero; (3) the pair part's coefficient at or above the calibrated 0.058 per point (1/(p(1-p)) per 100 at a 22% strikeout rate; the synthetic check ran 1.5 times calibrated, as ridge maps shrink); (4) the chain's league strikeout chance within 1.5 points of the observed rate and its walk chance within 2 points (the chain has no hit-by-pitch). Walks: the pair part's coefficient positive (MATCHUP-02 found chasing lowers walks). Decision rule: if (1) and (2) hold for strikeouts, the simulator gets a matchup layer from the engine's pair parts (strikeout and walk log-odds moved by the fitted coefficient times the pair points, maps and arsenals from earlier seasons only), replayed on 2025 and 2026 with paired streams against the production reference, then frozen and scored once on the untouched set; if only (1) holds, the effect is real but too small to move forecasts, the engine becomes a scouting view and contact quality is next; if (1) fails, strikeout matchups are additive in these maps and the program moves to batted-ball quality. Results (run 37880940236; 2025: 111,535 of 182,484 plate appearances in 50,727 pairs with maps and arsenals): the chain's league strikeout chance 23.9% against 22.2% observed (1.7 points high, prediction 4 missed by 0.2; the chain has no bunts, hit batters or catcher's interference and real counts do not run from the arsenal's mix), walks 7.6% against 9.0% (within 2). Strikeouts: SD of the engine deviation 2.1 points, hitter part 1.8, pitcher part 0.5, pair part 1.0 (shape 0.94, chain 0.24). **The pair part predicts strikeouts beyond both players' levels: coefficient +0.023 per point [0.008, 0.038], its shape part +0.021 [0.010, 0.034]**; out-of-sample gain +0.035 nats per 1,000 plate appearances [-0.018, +0.094]; the levels add nothing beyond the rates (-0.014). Walks: pair part +0.065 per point [0.039, 0.093], shape +0.069 [0.037, 0.093], gain +0.014 [-0.074, +0.085]. Across pair quintiles the observed strikeout rate runs 22.1% to 22.9% where the levels model says 22.4% at both ends; walks 8.8% to 9.9% against 9.3% at both ends. Predictions (1) and the walk sign held; (2) failed (the gain's interval includes zero); (3) failed: the coefficients are 40% (strikeouts) and 53% (walks) of calibrated, so the engine's pair deviations are about two and a half times too wide (maps tuned for pitches are noisy when summed over an arsenal). **Under the rule only (1) holds: the matchup effect is real but too small to move forecasts; no simulator layer; the engine becomes a scouting view (scaled by the fitted coefficients) and contact quality is next.** Measured on the way: what player averages hide about a specific matchup, from swing and miss maps, is a strikeout-rate SD of about 0.4 points and a walk-rate SD of about 0.35 points across pairs (pair part SD times coefficient over calibrated). | pair effect real (K +0.023/pt, BB +0.065/pt); too small for a layer |
| ENGINE-02 | ENGINE-01 with MATCHUP-05F's swing representation (the league with its family part, hitter family maps), as MATCHUP-05F's rule requires for the engine's pair parts: a slider-heavy arsenal against a hitter who chases sliders is a pitcher-hitter interaction a location-only map cannot see. Everything else as ENGINE-01 (2025 pairs, arsenals from 2024, maps from 2023-2024, whiff maps on the true crossing, the count chain, the two-way split, cross-fitted log loss, game bootstrap). Checked on synthetic seasons first with the family option: planted pair effects give a pair coefficient of 0.082 [0.070, 0.097] and a gain of 0.70 [0.35, 1.02]; with none, 0.024 [-0.019, +0.095] and 0.01 [-0.02, +0.04] | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: (1) the strikeout pair part's coefficient positive with the interval excluding zero; (2) the calibrated pair spread (pair SD times coefficient over the calibrated 0.058) larger than ENGINE-01's 0.40 points; (3) the pair part's out-of-sample gain positive with the interval excluding zero. Decision rule: if (1) and (3) hold, a simulator matchup layer becomes a candidate (replayed with paired streams, frozen, scored on the untouched months); if (1) and (2) hold without (3), the scouting view's matchup numbers use family maps and there is no simulator layer; if (1) fails, family maps add nothing to plate-appearance matchups. Results (run 37896232285; same 111,535 plate appearances in 50,727 pairs as ENGINE-01): strikeouts: pair part SD 1.07 points (ENGINE-01 1.00), **coefficient +0.019 per point [0.004, 0.031]** (ENGINE-01 +0.023), calibrated spread 0.35 points (ENGINE-01 0.40), out-of-sample gain +0.013 nats per 1,000 [-0.044, +0.069]; walks: pair part +0.060 [0.037, 0.088], gain +0.063 [-0.019, +0.132]. **(1) held, (2) and (3) failed: pitch-family maps do not enlarge what player averages hide about a plate-appearance matchup** (about 0.35 to 0.40 points of strikeout rate either way). No simulator layer; the scouting view keeps its pair numbers as they are (now from family maps, which change them little). | (1) held; (2), (3) failed: no larger pair effect |
| DISCIPLINE-01 | From the pitch-level discovery to the player model: do a hitter's decision-moment swing map and true-crossing whiff map, read on one standard set of pitches (40,000 from the same season, to his side, his own swing and whiff levels entering through the league models' propensity terms), project his next season's strikeouts and walks better than his own rates and raw plate-discipline numbers (chase rate, zone-swing rate, whiffs per swing, all shrunk)? Each source season's maps and league models fitted on that season only (feed, at least 600 pitches); targets every plate appearance of the next season; logistic models trained on 2023 to 2024 and scored on 2024 to 2025 and on 2025 to 2026 (through July), intervals clustered by hitter. Checked on synthetic seasons first (hitters seeing similar pitches): map and raw measures correlate 0.95 to 0.99 and the three map terms cost 0.09 to 0.21 nats per 1,000 plate appearances out of sample (fitting three hitter-level terms on 200 hitters), so a real effect has to clear that | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: the map terms gain on strikeouts with the interval excluding zero in both test pairs (a hitter's swing and miss tendencies measured free of the pitches he saw); walks reported. Decision rule: if the strikeout gain holds in both, the map measures enter the player model's batter features (a refit, then the replay gates); if not, the maps' value stays at the pitch and matchup level and the player model keeps its raw discipline numbers. Results (run 37883784534; 393, 400 and 388 hitters with maps in 2023, 2024 and 2025; 140,410 training plate appearances, 142,170 in 2025 and 97,881 in 2026 through July): the map measures correlate with the raw ones at 0.92 (chase), 0.90 (zone swing) and 0.98 (whiffs per swing). **Strikeouts: the map terms cost -0.37 nats per 1,000 plate appearances [-0.57, -0.18] (2025) and -0.49 [-0.74, -0.27] (2026)**; walks -0.03 and -0.02 (zero). **The prediction failed: under the rule the maps' value stays at the pitch and matchup level and the player model keeps its raw discipline numbers.** With DRIFT-01 (no early warning) and ENGINE-01 (pair effects about 0.4 points of strikeout rate), the pattern is consistent: the decision-moment representation predicts individual pitches far better (credited, MATCHUP-01F), but what it adds to a season's rates is already in the rates, and what it adds about a specific matchup is real and small. | failed: maps do not improve projections |
| EXPLOIT-01 | Do pitchers already aim at a hitter's own swing map? Hitter maps at the decision moment fitted on 2023-2024 (MATCHUP-01), read on 2025. For each 2025 hitter-pitcher pair and count group (two strikes, behind, other), the hitter's extra swing chance from his map on the pitches the pitcher actually threw him, against the same pitcher's 2025 pitches to other hitters of the same side in the same count group read through this hitter's map, with this hitter's own swing level (what he would have seen if pitched like everyone else). Outside the zone, a positive difference means more pitches where he chases; inside, aiming means more pitches where he takes. Reported in points of swing chance with intervals over hitters; whether it grows with how distinct the hitter's map is against that pitcher (slope on the generic deviation); and an oracle bound (the same pitcher's best pitches for him, as many as he was thrown), which picks extremes and so only bounds the headroom from above. Checked on synthetic seasons first: with pitchers aiming low at low-chasers, outside targeting +1.64 points [1.39, 1.86] and slope +0.25; with no aiming, -0.02 [-0.05, +0.01] and inside -0.01 (a first version compared pitches at other hitters' swing levels and showed a false +0.11 inside; fixed before registering) | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: outside the zone, targeting positive, 0.1 to 1.0 points, interval excluding zero (advance scouting aims a little at chase zones), and growing with the hitter's generic deviation (positive slope); inside the zone, reported. Decision rule: if outside targeting is under a quarter of the spread of hitters' generic chase deviations against the pitchers they face, most hitter-specific chase information is unexploited, and the scouting view lists each hitter's chase zones that pitchers are not reaching and the arsenals that reach them; if it is at least half, pitchers already use it and the view says so. Results (run 37884495989; 2025: 298,455 pitches outside the zone and 304,607 inside in 133,043 and 145,324 pair-count cells, 476 hitters with maps): **outside the zone, targeting -0.09 points [-0.14, -0.03]**: pitchers put slightly fewer pitches where a hitter personally chases than they put there for other hitters, against a spread of hitters' generic chase deviations of 3.65 points; slope on the generic deviation -0.01 (no aiming at more distinct hitters); inside the zone -0.02 [-0.05, +0.02]; the oracle bound 14.4 points (an upper bound only). **The prediction failed (no targeting at all)**. Under the rule (targeting under a quarter of the spread), the hitter-specific chase information in the decision-moment maps is unexploited: pitchers' locations against a hitter do not follow his own map, so the scouting view lists each hitter's chase zones and the pitchers whose arsenals reach them. Caveat: pitchers may aim at weaknesses described another way (pitch types, true-crossing heat maps), which these maps beat for predicting swings (MATCHUP-01F) but which would not show as targeting here. | no targeting (-0.09 pts): map information unexploited |
| EXPLOIT-02 | Do pitchers aim at a hitter's own whiff holes? EXPLOIT-01 found no aiming at decision-moment swing maps. Misses follow where the ball arrives (MATCHUP-03), and whiff holes are what scouting reports describe, so the league may already aim at them. Both maps as each hitter's own part (his map minus the same-side mean map of the other hitters, VALUE-08): the whiff map on the true crossing (MATCHUP-03's representation, shrinkage 30, at least 250 swings) and the swing map at the decision moment (MATCHUP-05F's). Maps from 2023-2024; for each 2025 hitter-pitcher pair, zone side and count group, the own-part deviation at the pitches thrown to him against the same pitcher's pitches to other hitters of that side read through his maps at his own levels; headroom from the best of the same pool; bootstrap over hitters. Checked on synthetic seasons first: with pitchers aiming higher at hitters who miss high, whiff targeting +0.89 points [0.76, 1.04] outside the zone (11% of the headroom) and +0.25 [0.19, 0.29] in it, chase targeting nil (-0.04 [-0.21, +0.12]); with no aiming, all nil (0.00 [-0.02, +0.02]) | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: whiff targeting positive with the interval excluding zero both outside and inside the zone, using at least 5% of the headroom; chase targeting's interval includes zero on both sides (as EXPLOIT-01). Decision rule: if both hold, the league already aims at where a hitter misses but not at where he chases at the decision moment, which the scouting view states; whiff holes then cannot be priced by this natural experiment. If whiff holes are not targeted either, VALUE-12 prices the whiff own part with VALUE-08's design. Results (run 37894956341; 2025, 439 hitters with whiff maps, 476 with swing maps): **whiff holes: targeting +0.153 points [0.112, 0.193] outside the zone, 1.6% of the headroom (9.4 points); +0.091 [0.063, 0.121] in the zone, 1.3% of 7.1.** Chase spots (own part): outside -0.082 [-0.140, -0.029], -0.5% of 16.0; inside -0.021 [-0.060, +0.017]. Predictions: whiff targeting positive held, but its share of the headroom (1.3 to 1.6%) fell short of 5%; chase targeting nil held inside and failed outside (slightly negative, as EXPLOIT-01's -0.09). **Reading: pitchers lean very slightly toward a hitter's own whiff holes and very slightly away from his own chase spots; both use almost none of what is available.** Under the rule, whiff holes are close enough to untargeted for VALUE-08's design (its placebo, within-hitter and same-spot checks guard the rest): VALUE-12 prices them. | whiff +0.15 pts (1.6% of headroom); chase -0.08 (none) |
| EXPLOIT-03 | Is the edge left on the table even in October? EXPLOIT-01 found no regular-season aiming at hitters' own chase spots (-0.09 points), EXPLOIT-02 a little at whiff holes; postseason staffs prepare for one lineup with far more advance scouting. EXPLOIT-01's design with hitter maps at the decision moment fitted on the 2023-2025 regular seasons (MATCHUP-01, shrinkage 10) and targeting measured on every 2023, 2024 and 2025 postseason pitch (feed, game types F, D, L and W; development data, the 2026 postseason stays untouched). For each postseason hitter-pitcher pair, count group and zone side: the hitter's extra swing chance from his map on the pitches the pitcher threw him, against the same pitcher's pitches to other same-side hitters read through this hitter's map at his own swing level. Two comparison pools: (a) the pitcher's postseason pitches of that year to other hitters (pure targeting within October); (b) his regular-season pitches of that year to other hitters (October's pitching to this hitter against how he pitched everyone all season). Intervals over hitters. Checked on synthetic seasons first, with one game in sixteen marked postseason: aiming only in October gives +1.99 points [1.70, 2.26] with pool (a) and +1.97 [1.71, 2.21] with pool (b); no aiming gives -0.01 [-0.11, +0.09] and +0.02 [-0.03, +0.09] | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: outside the zone, postseason targeting is under 0.3 points with the interval including zero, with both pools (no aiming at these spots in October either). Decision rule: if either pool shows targeting of at least 0.3 points with the interval excluding zero, the scouting view says postseason staffs partly reach hitters' own chase spots and how much of the headroom they use; otherwise it says the edge is unexploited even in the postseason. EXPLOIT-03F repeats this once on the 2026 postseason after the World Series Results (runs 37919178737 and 37919307147; 38,403 postseason pitches, 11,740 in 2023, 12,684 in 2024 and 13,979 in 2025; maps from 2,120,398 regular-season pitches): **outside the zone, (a) postseason pool +0.042 points [-0.134, +0.209] (9,200 pitches to 282 hitters; 0.4% of a 10.1-point headroom); (b) regular-season pool +0.045 [-0.086, +0.175] (19,052 pitches, 298 hitters; 0.3% of 14.8 points)**; inside -0.075 and -0.070, both spanning zero; no growth with how distinct the hitter's map is (slopes -0.05 and -0.00). **The prediction held with both pools: postseason staffs do not aim at hitters' own chase spots either.** Under the rule the scouting view says the edge is unexploited even in the postseason (2023-2025); EXPLOIT-03F checks 2026 after the World Series. | held: no aiming in October either (+0.04 pts) |

The whiff own part carries chase structure (maps are fitted on swings, and where a hitter swings follows his chase map), which the chase controls do not fully remove; with a planted effect the estimate was also too noisy to recover it (-0.000213 [-0.000532, +0.000042] against a true -0.00013). Pricing whiff holes needs a different design, for example a structural one (calibrated whiff maps times the run value of a miss against contact in each count), not this natural experiment. | Synthetic checks only (scratchpad matchup/value12_smoke.py, value12_null.py). | not run: synthetic null failed |

| VALUE-01 | What a hitter's own swing map is worth to a pitcher, in runs. Pitchers do not aim at it (EXPLOIT-01), so where a pitch falls on the hitter's map, beyond the league's map at the same spot, is as good as chance with respect to the rest of the plate appearance: a natural experiment. Every 2025 pitch to a hitter with a map (fitted on 2023-2024): d = his extra swing chance there, in points. The plate appearance's run value (linear weights of its final outcome) on d outside and inside the zone, holding the count (twelve categories), the league's swing chance at that spot and its log-odds, zone-distance bands, pitch group, both players' earlier run values and platoon fixed; game-clustered bootstrap. Placebo: d from another hitter's map of the same side. Worth of aiming: within each pitcher's own outside pitches to that side and count group, and within thirds of the league swing chance (so the spot stays as good in general), his best third for this hitter against the average, times the outside coefficient and the outside pitches per plate appearance, scaled to 6,200 plate appearances (a team's season). Checked on synthetic seasons first (strikeouts driven by chases, no aiming by pitchers): planted hitter-specific zones give -0.00031 runs per point outside [-0.00050, -0.00005], placebo +0.00003, aiming -19.6 runs per 6,200 [-32.4, -2.9]; with no hitter-specific zones +0.00009 and +2.6 [-14.7, +17.4] (one of the null placebo intervals excluded zero, -0.00063, the expected rate of chance excursions over the six intervals) | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: (1) the own-map outside coefficient negative with the interval excluding zero (a hitter does worse when pitches land where he personally chases), the placebo's interval including zero; inside reported; (2) the worth of aiming negative with the interval excluding zero, between -5 and -40 runs per 6,200 plate appearances. Decision rule: if (1) holds, the maps carry run value for pitchers, sized by (2); the estimate is frozen and scored once on the untouched set (maps from 2023-2025, the 2026 pitches from August 1) before any figure is stated outside the lab, and the scouting view carries it; if (1) fails, a hitter's chase zones do not cost him runs beyond the league's map, and the scouting value is descriptive. Results (run 37885781639; 617,627 pitches of 2025, 49.5% outside the zone; the hitter's own deviation d has SD 7.8 points outside and 7.3 inside): **outside the zone -0.000694 runs per point [-0.000901, -0.000526]**: a pitch that lands where this hitter personally chases, by 10 points more than the league, costs him 0.007 runs over the plate appearance; inside the zone +0.000826 [0.000629, 0.001037] (a strike where he swings more than the league helps him). Placebo with another hitter's map: outside -0.000124 [-0.000298, +0.000091], inside -0.000025: the effect is his own map's. Aiming: the best third of each pitcher's own outside spots for this hitter, within thirds of the league's swing chance, is 6.1 points above their average; with 1.93 outside pitches per plate appearance, **-0.0081 runs per plate appearance [-0.0106, -0.0062], -50.4 runs per 6,200 [-65.4, -38.2]**. Prediction (1) held; (2) failed by size (beyond the predicted -5 to -40). **Under the rule the estimate is frozen and scored once on the untouched set (VALUE-01F) before any figure is stated outside the lab.** Reading, with its limits: this is what perfect placement within each pitcher's own outside spots would be worth (real command scatters pitches around the aim, so a realistic share is lower), it counts outside pitches only (moving strikes to where a hitter swings less would add the inside term), and hitters could adapt once pitched this way. Even a fifth of it is about 10 runs a season, and EXPLOIT-01 says nobody is collecting it. | -0.00069 runs/pt outside; aiming up to -50 runs/season; untouched test next |
| VALUE-01F | VALUE-01 scored once on the untouched set: hitter maps and the league map at the decision moment fitted on 2023-2025, every 2026 pitch from August 1 (feed, locations as reported, ZONE-02), the same regression, placebo and aiming calculation. Code: value_study with final_eval in tools/brl_discovery.py, frozen in the commit that adds this row (the development path is unchanged; the final path refuses to run without a frozen commit). Checked on synthetic seasons relabeled as the untouched months: the planted values are reproduced exactly (-0.000305 per point, aiming -19.6 runs per 6,200) | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run; one scoring.** Predictions on the untouched pitches: (a) the own-map outside coefficient negative with the interval excluding zero, the placebo's interval including zero; (b) the worth of aiming negative with the interval excluding zero, between -20 and -80 runs per 6,200 plate appearances; (c) the inside coefficient positive. Decision rule: if (a) and (b) hold, it is credited: a hitter's own decision-moment chase map is worth up to that many runs a season to pitchers able to place each outside pitch at that hitter's best spots among their own, an upper bound under perfect placement, and no pitcher is collecting it (EXPLOIT-01); the scouting view states it with that bound. If (a) fails, the development estimate is not confirmed. Results (run 37886038190, frozen commit 63623e0f; 184,318 untouched pitches to hitters with maps, August 1 to September 27, 2026; d SD 8.1 points outside, 7.5 inside): **(a) outside -0.000946 runs per point [-0.00122, -0.00061]**, placebo -0.000186 [-0.000535, +0.000173]; **(c) inside +0.000384 [0.000025, 0.000843]**, placebo -0.000132; **(b) aiming: best third 6.3 points above the average of each pitcher's own outside spots, -0.0111 runs per plate appearance [-0.0143, -0.0071], -68.8 runs per 6,200 [-88.7, -44.2]**. Every prediction held: credited. Stated with its bound: a hitter's own decision-moment chase map, fitted on earlier seasons, tells a pitcher where among his own outside spots that hitter is most likely to chase, and on data no model had seen, putting pitches there cost hitters runs at a rate that, if every outside pitch could be placed at the best third of those spots, would be worth about 69 runs over a team's season (44 to 89). Perfect placement is not real command: the realistic share is the next measurement (VALUE-02, command scatter). No pitcher is collecting it today (EXPLOIT-01). | credited on untouched data: up to 69 runs a season under perfect placement; run figure withdrawn (VALUE-07 placebo), replaced by VALUE-08F |
| VALUE-02 | VALUE-01 with real command (development, 2025). Each aim point (one of the pitcher's own 2025 spots to that side and count group) is scattered by command error, a two-dimensional normal offset with SD sigma per axis (the decision-moment picture shifts with it), and the hitter's own deviation is averaged over the scatter; the pitcher takes the best third of his aim points for the hitter by that expected deviation (outside the zone the highest; inside the lowest, since there a higher deviation helps the hitter), within thirds of the league's swing chance; the gain times the run value per point (VALUE-01's regression re-estimated on the same pitches) and the pitches per plate appearance. Sigma 0, 0.3, 0.6, 0.9 ft (typical misses from a catcher's target run about a foot, near 0.6 to 0.8 per axis). A pitch that scatters across the zone edge keeps the coefficient of where it was aimed. Checked on synthetic seasons first: planted, the outside gain falls from 4.9 points at sigma 0 (-19.7 runs per 6,200, matching VALUE-01's synthetic -19.6) to 2.6 at 0.9 (-10.5), outside and inside together -29.8 to -15.1; null, +6.4 to +2.4 | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: at sigma 0 the outside-only value reproduces VALUE-01's -50 within 5 runs; at sigma 0.6 the outside-only value keeps 40 to 75 percent of it; outside and inside together at sigma 0.6 between -15 and -50 runs per 6,200 plate appearances. Decision rule: development measurement of the realistic edge; if outside and inside together at sigma 0.6 is beyond -10 runs (a win or more a season), the frozen version gets its own scoring on the untouched set (a second look at those months, registered as such) before it is stated outside the lab; otherwise the edge is stated as the perfect-placement bound only. Results (run 37886531947; the same 2025 pitches, coefficients -0.000694 outside and +0.000827 inside per point; 1.93 outside and 1.97 inside pitches per plate appearance): runs per 6,200 plate appearances, outside only / inside only / both: **sigma 0: -50.4 / -57.8 / -108.2; 0.3: -41.8 / -48.1 / -89.9; 0.6: -31.1 / -34.4 / -65.6; 0.9: -23.8 / -26.7 / -50.5** (gains outside 6.1, 5.0, 3.7, 2.9 points; inside -5.7, -4.8, -3.4, -2.6). Predictions: sigma 0 reproduced VALUE-01 (held); outside at 0.6 kept 62 percent (held); both at 0.6 -65.6, beyond the predicted -15 to -50 (failed by size). Under the rule (beyond -10), the frozen version gets a second look at the untouched months (VALUE-02F). Caveats carried forward: the inside coefficient on the untouched months was half its development size (VALUE-01F: +0.00038 against +0.00083), so the inside part is the less certain half; ADAPT-01 found hitters chase less at all outside pitches after being shown more tempting ones than chance, which this calculation does not net out. | realistic command (0.6 ft): -31 outside, -66 with inside; untouched look next |

Decision rule: if (a) holds, the realistic outside edge is credited with its interval (the statement keeps ADAPT-01's caveat: hitters tighten everywhere outside after seeing more tempting pitches than chance, not netted here); the inside part is credited only if (b) holds and the inside coefficient's interval excludes zero there. Results (run 37887201588, frozen commit 61549d73; 184,318 untouched pitches; coefficients -0.000945 outside and +0.000384 inside per point; 1.88 outside and 2.04 inside pitches per plate appearance): runs per 6,200 plate appearances, outside only / both, with intervals: sigma 0: -68.7 [-91.6, -42.4] / -97.7 [-140.1, -69.4]; 0.3: -57.0 [-76.0, -35.2] / -81.0 [-116.3, -57.6]; **0.6: -42.5 [-56.7, -26.2] / -59.6 [-85.2, -42.5]**; 0.9: -32.4 [-43.2, -20.0] / -45.6 [-65.2, -32.5]. **(a) and (b) held, and the inside coefficient's interval excludes zero on these months (VALUE-01F: [0.000025, 0.000843]): credited.** Stated: with typical command (misses about 0.6 ft per axis), aiming each outside pitch at the best third of the pitcher's own spots for that hitter's decision-moment chase map is worth about 43 runs over a team's season (26 to 57) on data no model had seen, and aiming strikes at the spots where that hitter swings least adds about 17 more; nobody does it today (EXPLOIT-01). Not netted out: ADAPT-01's general tightening (hitters chase less everywhere outside after seeing more tempting pitches than chance in a game), longer-run adaptation if a team pitched this way all season, and any cost of steering location choices away from other aims (sequencing, pitch selection). | VALUE-03 | VALUE-02's outside edge at 0.6 ft of command scatter, net of ADAPT-01's general tightening (development, 2025): each 2025 outside pitch to a hitter with a map keeps its pitcher, side and count group; aiming at the best third of that pitcher's own spots under scatter gives a gain in the hitter's deviation and a share of pitches landing in his high spots; walking each hitter-game in order, the expected extra high spots seen before each pitch, beyond his own share, lower the log-odds of his swinging by 0.065 each (ADAPT-01). Checked on synthetic seasons first: with a planted breaking-ball shift the family maps' gain in points rises from 2.84 to 4.18; the placebo's intervals include zero in every world; with no family effect the family maps' larger point gains are offset by a smaller coefficient (outside -6.3 against -7.3 runs per 6,200), so lighter shrinkage does not inflate the value (that synthetic outcome effect is weak, so its run intervals include zero for both kinds of map) | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: (1) the outside value at 0.6 ft at least 10% larger than VALUE-05's -26.3 runs per 6,200 (beyond -28.9); (2) the placebo's outside coefficient interval includes zero; (3) the within-hitter coefficient's outside interval excludes zero. Decision rule: if all three hold, family maps become the aiming basis of the scouting view once MATCHUP-04F holds, and the frozen version is scored once on the untouched months (VALUE-06F, registered as another look at them); if (1) fails, family maps predict decisions better but do not add aiming value, and aiming stays on location maps; if (2) or (3) fails, the run value per point is questioned and VALUE-02F's reading is re-examined. Prediction (1) held, (2) failed, (3) held. Reading: at light shrinkage every hitter's family part carries a shared family-by-location pattern the league model lacks; that shared part predicts outcomes for any hitter, and a league-wide pattern is something pitchers can and do aim at, so it is neither hitter-specific nor as good as chance. **Under the rule the run value per point is questioned: the family-map value (-40) is not usable as is.** Next: VALUE-07 re-examines VALUE-02F's reading on location maps with the placebo, the within-hitter check and a new check that one pitch's coefficient is not counting its neighbors in the plate appearance; the family version is redone against a league with its own family-by-location part. | failed (placebo -0.000375, CI excl 0) |

Decision rule: if (a)'s neighbor-held coefficient is under 80% of the plain one, VALUE-01F and VALUE-02F overstate the value by that ratio; they are corrected by re-scoring the untouched months with the neighbor-held coefficient (registered as a correction look) and every page number moves to it; if (a)'s placebo fails, the location-map value is withdrawn pending a cleaner design. If (b) holds, family maps become the aiming basis once MATCHUP-05 holds, scored once on the untouched months (VALUE-07F). Results: (a) run 37891566929, location maps: own map outside -0.000694 runs per point [-0.00094, -0.00048] (aiming -26.3 runs per 6,200 [-35.7, -18.2]); **placebo -0.000267 [-0.000491, -0.000088]** (-10.1 runs [-18.6, -3.3]); within hitter, pitch group and zone side -0.000576 [-0.000763, -0.000388] (-21.8); the outside deviations correlate 0.161 within a plate appearance; holding the plate appearance's other pitches fixed -0.000609 [-0.000754, -0.000490] (88% of the plain coefficient, -23.1 runs), with the within-hitter deviation as well -0.000571 (-21.7). (b) run 37891586906, family maps against the league with its family part: own -0.000647 [-0.000811, -0.000499] (-31.9 [-40.0, -24.6]); **placebo -0.000221 [-0.000366, -0.000066]** (-10.9); within -0.000509 (-25.1); correlation 0.136; neighbor-held -0.000558 (86%, -27.5 runs); within and neighbor-held -0.000496 (-24.5). **(a): the neighbor check held (88%); the placebo failed. (b): the placebo failed.** Reading: the placebo is one random reassignment of maps (VALUE-01's reassignment on the same 2025 pitches gave -0.000124 [-0.000298, +0.000091]); both point the same way, so hitters' maps share a shape that predicts run value for anyone beyond the league model. That shared part is not hitter-specific and is not shown to be as good as chance (pitchers can aim at a shape every hitter shares). **Under the rule the location-map run value is withdrawn pending a cleaner design: VALUE-01F and VALUE-02F stay recorded as run, and their run figures are not stated until VALUE-08 (each hitter's own part, net of the same-side mean map of the other hitters) is scored.** The swing-prediction credits (MATCHUP-01F, MATCHUP-04F) are unaffected. | (a) placebo failed, neighbors 88%; (b) placebo failed; run values withdrawn pending VALUE-08 |

The regression carries both; the run value per point, the checks (the placebo with a reassigned map net of the same mean map, plus five more reassignments; within hitter, pitch group and zone side; neighbor-held) and the aiming (the best third of the pitcher's spots by the hitter's own part) use only the own part. They resample games, but a hitter's map and a pitcher's habits span many games, and errors shared within a hitter or a pitcher would make game intervals too narrow (the VALUE-12 null worlds showed across-hitter estimates are vulnerable; the within-hitter primary should not be). Two runs: 2025 (maps 2023-2024) and 2026 through July (maps 2023-2025). Checked on synthetic seasons first: with strikeouts driven by chases, the strikeout coefficient comes out positive (+0.0026 per point [0.0020, 0.0034]), outs in play negative, and the weighted sum equals the run-value coefficient | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the runs.** Predictions: outside the zone, the own part raises strikeouts and lowers walks (both intervals excluding zero), and those two channels carry at least half of the run value in both seasons. Decision rule: descriptive; the scouting view says which outcomes the edge moves. Results (runs 37900252781 and 37900274732), per point of the own part on an outside pitch: **walks -0.00155 [-0.00171, -0.00138] in 2025 and -0.00168 [-0.00190, -0.00145] in 2026; outs in play +0.00094 and +0.00102; strikeouts -0.00001 [-0.00025, +0.00026] and +0.00023 [-0.00002, +0.00045]**; singles +0.00043 and +0.00027, doubles and triples +0.00012 and +0.00008. In runs: walks -0.00050 and -0.00054, outs in play -0.00025 and -0.00027, singles and extra-base hits give back +0.00030 and +0.00019; the weighted sum equals the run-value coefficient (-0.000385, -0.000637). **The prediction failed on strikeouts: pitches in a hitter's own chase spots take away his walks and turn takes into weak contact; they do not add strikeouts.** The scouting view says so. | failed in part: walks and outs in play carry it, not strikeouts |
| ABS-01 | Why the chase-spot value grew in 2026 (VALUE-11). Candidate mechanism: with the 2026 ball-strike challenge system a taken pitch outside the zone is called a ball more reliably, so a chase gives up more against a take. Development data (2025 and 2026 through July): the called-strike chance of taken pitches by distance from the zone edge (inside and outside, in inches), by season, overall and at the side edges only (heights 2.0 to 3.0 ft, where the plate's width is the edge in both seasons and the 2026 zone's batter-specific top and bottom do not enter); and the plate appearance's run value for a swing against a take on pitches outside the zone by season (count, distance band, pitch group, both players' earlier run values, platoon), with the 2026 change's game-bootstrap interval. Checked on synthetic seasons first: a planted 2026 drop in called strikes just outside is recovered (0.38 to 0.22 within an inch); the run-value change is underpowered there (planted +0.002 [-0.002, +0.008], null +0.004 [-0.000, +0.010]), so it is reported, not tested | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Prediction: at the side edges, taken pitches 0 to 1 inch and 1 to 2 inches outside are called strikes at least 2 points less often in 2026 than in 2025. Reading set in advance: if it holds, the challenge system plausibly raised the price of a chase and the 2026 value is described with that mechanism as a candidate; if not, the 2026 increase stays unexplained. Results (run 37896872242): **at the side edges, taken pitches 0 to 1 inch outside were called strikes 49.0% of the time in 2025 and 34.8% in 2026; 1 to 2 inches outside 25.9% and 15.4%; 2 to 4 inches 6.2% and 2.7%.** Just inside, 0 to 1 inch 73.5% and 71.2%, 1 to 2 inches 89.5% and 93.0%: the called zone moved toward the rulebook edge. All edges together: 0 to 1 inch outside 33.0% and 20.3%. A swing on a pitch outside the zone, against a take in the same count, distance band and pitch group, cost -0.112 runs per plate appearance [-0.115, -0.109] in 2025 and -0.123 [-0.126, -0.120] in 2026, a change of -0.011 [-0.016, -0.007]. **The prediction held.** Reading: the 2026 challenge system took away most of the borderline strikes a hitter used to get for taking, so chasing costs more; this is a candidate part of why a hitter's own chase spots are worth more in 2026, but the price of a chase rose about 10% while the per-point value rose about 65%, so it does not account for all of it. | passed: edge strikes on takes -14 points; chase cost -10% |
| ABS-02 | Who the 2026 challenge system would hurt, measured in 2025 (a rule change as a shock with exposure measured before it): each pitcher's and hitter's 2025 taken pitches by distance from the zone edge, times ABS-01's change in called-strike chance in that band, per plate appearance, against his 2025-to-2026 change in walk rate, strikeout rate and run value. Not run: **the synthetic check showed the design cannot see a real effect.** With 200 pitchers at about 400 plate appearances a season and the walk effect planted, the coefficient was +0.084 [-0.033, +0.205]: exposure varies across pitchers by about 0.02 strikes per plate appearance, worth about 0.002 of walk rate per standard deviation, against season-to-season walk-rate noise of about 0.015. | Synthetic check only (scratchpad matchup/abs2_smoke.py). | not run: underpowered by design |
| FRAMING-01 | What the 2026 challenge system did to catcher framing (ABS-01 found it took away borderline strikes; framed strikes are what a challenge overturns). Feed pitch table joined to the plate-appearance history for the catcher (the history's fielder_2 by game and at-bat); every taken pitch within 2 inches of the zone edge, either side; called-strike chance by half-inch band and season (league); each take's surplus split into catcher and pitcher parts by backfitting within a season; catchers with at least 800 such takes in a season; spread of catcher parts by season (extra strikes per 1,000 borderline takes) and persistence from one season to the next (2024 to 2025 against 2025 to 2026), intervals from resampling catchers. Development data (2026 through July). Checked on synthetic seasons first: framing planted at 30% strength in 2026 gives a persistence ratio of 0.34 and a spread ratio of 0.36; unchanged framing 1.05 and 1.03 | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: the spread of catcher framing in 2026 at most 0.8 of 2025's, and the 2025-to-2026 persistence slope at most 0.8 of the 2024-to-2025 slope. Decision rule: descriptive, for scouting catchers (framing's worth under the challenge system); if neither falls, challenges have not re-priced framing. Results (run 37905456580; every take matched to a catcher; borderline takes 79,673, 80,459 and 56,548): called-strike rate on them 50.5%, 44.7% and 37.1%; **spread of catcher framing 36.4, 40.0 and 26.9 extra strikes per 1,000 borderline takes (2026 over 2025: 0.67)**; persistence slope 0.32 [-0.06, 0.80] from 2024 to 2025 (33 catchers) and 0.16 [-0.08, 0.33] from 2025 to 2026 (25), ratio 0.48. **Both predictions held, the second only as a point estimate** (the persistence intervals overlap; this simple catcher measure is noisy even between 2024 and 2025). Reading: the challenge system cut the spread of framing by about a third, and that is conservative, since 2026's shorter sample adds noise that would widen the spread; at about 0.13 runs a strike, a catcher one SD above average was worth about 5 runs per 1,000 borderline takes in 2025 and about 3.5 in 2026. | passed: framing spread 0.67x in 2026 |
| BATSPEED-01 | What changes before results do, in the swing itself (DRIFT-01 found swing decisions give no early warning). A hitter's mean bat speed over his last 14 days against his own norm (the 120 days before that), competitive swings only (at least 50 mph), at least 25 recent and 150 norm swings, as a predictor of each plate appearance's run value (linear weights of its event) beyond his earlier and last-14-day run values (shrunk), his strikeout rate, the pitcher's earlier run value allowed, platoon and his norm bat speed. Savant 2025 (fit) and 2026 through July (scored); squared-error reduction per 1,000 plate appearances by game; coefficient interval from refits on resampled training games; observed against the base model by deviation band. Checked on synthetic seasons first: a planted drift effect (0.03 runs per mph of true drift) is recovered as 0.0139 per mph of measured deviation [0.0123, 0.0161] with a gain of 0.21 [0.16, 0.26]; with none, -0.0003 [-0.0019, +0.0020] and 0.00 | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: (1) the coefficient positive (a hitter swinging slower than his norm produces less) with the interval excluding zero, 0.002 to 0.010 runs per plate appearance per mph; (2) the squared-error reduction on 2026 positive with the interval excluding zero. Decision rule: if both hold, bat speed against norm becomes a recent-form input candidate for the player model (a refit and the replay gates) and a scouting flag, frozen and scored once on the untouched months first; if not, short-term bat-speed changes do not run ahead of results either. Results (run 37888985847; 111,564 training and 62,261 test plate appearances; deviation SD 0.98 mph): **+0.0019 runs per plate appearance per mph [-0.0002, +0.0043]; squared-error reduction on 2026 +0.007 per 1,000 [-0.005, +0.021]**; fitted on 2026 alone +0.0029. By band on 2026: 1.5 mph or more below norm -0.016 against the base model (1,851 plate appearances), 1.5 or more above +0.010 (6,163), the middle bands flat. **Both predictions failed (the intervals include zero).** Under the rule, short-term bat-speed changes do not run ahead of results at a useful size either: only the extremes lean the expected way. With DRIFT-01, the early-warning question is answered no for both swing decisions and bat speed at two-week resolution. | failed: +0.0019/mph (CI incl 0) |
| ADAPT-01 | Would hitters adapt if pitchers aimed at their own chase spots? Pitchers do not aim today (EXPLOIT-01), so how many of a hitter's high spots he has already been shown in a game is chance. Every 2025 pitch outside the zone to a hitter with a map (2023-2024): his swing, with the map's prediction as offset, on the number of earlier pitches in the game that fell in his high spots (outside, his own deviation in the top quarter) beyond what his own share of high spots gives by chance among the outside pitches he has seen, and its product with this pitch's deviation (does his excess chasing at his own spots shrink after he has been shown them?), with outside pitches seen, his share of high spots times deviation, outside pitches seen times deviation, earlier high spots chased (secondary: a hacking day raises both) and their product, earlier plate appearances and pitch number. Game-bootstrap intervals. Checked on synthetic games first: with adaptation planted (15 percent of the excess lost per earlier high spot) the key product is -0.083 [-0.099, -0.068]; with none -0.010 [-0.021, +0.002] (a first version that counted high spots without the chance adjustment showed a false +0.017 with none, because hitters with more distinct maps have more high spots; fixed before registering) | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Prediction: the key product (high spots seen beyond chance times deviation) negative with the interval excluding zero: hitters lose some of their excess chasing at their own spots after being shown them within a game. Decision rule: if it holds, the realistic edge (VALUE-02) is discounted for repetition within a game and the scouting view says to spread the pitches into a hitter's spots across the game; if the interval includes zero or is above it, the edge does not wear off within a game at today's exposure levels. Results (run 37886804864; 305,771 outside pitches of 2025, high spots above 3.2 points): **the key product, high spots seen beyond chance times deviation, +0.009 [-0.004, +0.024]: hitters do not lose their excess chasing at their own spots after being shown them.** But the main term is clearly negative: **each extra high spot seen beyond chance lowers the log-odds of chasing any outside pitch by 0.065 [0.054, 0.074]** (about 1.4 points of chase rate at a 30 percent base), a general tightening rather than learning the spots; outside pitches seen -0.015 per pitch; earlier high spots chased +0.082 (a hacking day raises both, as expected); earlier plate appearances +0.051; pitch number -0.025. The prediction failed. Under the rule the spot-specific edge does not wear off within a game; the general tightening is an unregistered finding that bears on any aiming strategy: a pitcher who aims at a hitter's spots shows him more of them than chance, and each extra one costs about 1.4 points of chasing everywhere outside, so repeated aiming within a game gives back part of the gain. Netting the two needs its own registration (VALUE-03). | no spot learning; general tightening -0.065 per extra spot |

ADAPT-01 found no learning of own spots within a game; this asks across games. Decision rule: if it holds, the scouting view says the edge holds across a series; if the coefficient is below -0.02 with the interval excluding zero, aim values are discounted by the fade for repeated meetings. Results (run 37901940710; 305,771 outside pitches, 65,571 after at least one earlier meeting, mean 0.5 meetings; own part SD 0.52 log-odds): **own part times earlier meetings -0.011 per meeting [-0.026, +0.008]**; at the hitter's highest own spots the observed swing runs 0.395, 0.399 and 0.407 after 0, 1-3 and 4-9 earlier meetings against map predictions of 0.427, 0.429 and 0.436 (a constant shortfall, no growing one). **The prediction held: a hitter does not learn a pitcher's way into his own chase spots over a season's meetings.** The scouting view says the edge holds across a series. | passed: -0.011 [-0.026, +0.008] per meeting |
| DAMAGE-01 | The third part of the matchup engine: where a hitter does damage, and which clock it follows. Each 2023-2025 ball in play's expected run value from its launch speed and angle (a league table of run value by speed and angle bins fitted on 2023-2024, so fielding and luck on where it lands drop out). League model: location bands by count, pitch group, speed, platoon and the hitter's earlier damage (shrunk), linear. Hitter damage maps: ridge regression of the residual on the 22-column hitter basis (at least 150 training balls in play), shrinkage chosen on the second half of 2024 from 10 to 10,000. Each on the true crossing and at the decision moment; trained 2023-2024, scored on 2025, squared-error reduction per 1,000 balls in play, paired by game; calibration across quintiles of the map deviation. Checked on synthetic balls in play first: planted hot zones (up in the zone, SD 3 mph of launch speed) gain 0.100 [0.061, 0.138] on the true crossing and 0.041 at the decision moment (true better by 0.061 [0.023, 0.097]), calibrated across quintiles; with none, the validation picks the largest shrinkage and the maps gain -0.0004 [-0.0026, +0.0017] (a first grid that stopped at 300 overfit the null at -0.10; widened before registering) | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: (1) hitter damage maps on the true crossing reduce the squared error on 2025 with the interval excluding zero; (2) the true crossing beats the decision moment for damage maps, interval excluding zero; (3) observed damage beyond the league model follows the map deviation across quintiles. Decision rule: if (1) holds, damage maps join the matchup engine (swings at the decision moment; misses and damage at the true crossing) and the scouting view gets a damage grid; (2) holding extends the two-clock reading to contact quality. If (1) fails, a hitter's damage is his level plus the league's map, and the engine's contact value comes from the league. Results (run 37886945666; 123,561 balls in play of 2025, 79.8% by hitters with a map; expected run value SD 0.309): squared error per ball in play, league 0.091977 and hitter maps 0.091770 on the true crossing (shrinkage 300), 0.093466 and 0.093432 at the decision moment (1,000). **(1) Hitter damage maps on the true crossing reduce it by 0.207 per 1,000 [0.151, 0.260]; (2) the true crossing beats the decision moment for hitter maps by 1.66 per 1,000 [1.53, 1.79]** (the league model alone is better on the true crossing by 1.49); (3) across quintiles of the map deviation the observed damage beyond the league runs -0.011, 0.000, +0.006, +0.011, +0.025 against map deviations -0.016, -0.002, +0.001, +0.006, +0.023. **Every prediction held: damage maps join the matchup engine on the true crossing, and the two-clock reading extends to contact quality: swing decisions follow where the pitch appears to be headed 260 ms out, misses and damage follow where it actually arrives.** The hitter maps' share of the variance is small (0.2 percent of the squared error: most of a ball in play's value is the swing itself), which is why they help most as a map of where a hitter does damage, not as a forecaster of single balls in play. A one-time scoring on the untouched months is registered as DAMAGE-01F. | damage maps +0.21 (true crossing); true beats decision moment by 1.66 |

| FATIGUE-01 | Matchup program M3, the pitcher's state inside the game. Starters' plate appearances 2023 to July 2026 from the feed (the program's untouched set dropped in code): drift = mean of his last eight fastballs (four-seam and sinker) before the plate appearance minus his first ten of the game, for speed, height at 50 ft, spin and extension (defined once he has thrown 18); start against norm = his first ten fastballs' speed minus his fastball speed on earlier days. Controls: pitch count (bands), times this batter has faced him (2nd, 3rd or later), both players' earlier rates by outcome class (shrunk), platoon. Multinomial outcome models fitted on 2023-2024, scored on 2025 and 2026 through July, paired by game; run value per plate appearance on the same features with game-clustered intervals; the times-through-the-order terms with and without drift. Checked on synthetic games first: a planted speed effect is recovered (drift -0.023 runs per mph, start -0.020), none gives -0.003 and 0.000 | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: drift in fastball speed costs 0.004 to 0.015 runs per plate appearance per mph lost, interval excluding zero; a start below his usual speed costs 0.005 to 0.020 runs per plate appearance per mph for the whole game, interval excluding zero; start against norm improves out-of-sample log loss by at least 0.3 nats per 1,000 plate appearances in 2025 with the interval excluding zero; adding drift shrinks the third-time-through penalty by at least 15 percent. Decision rule: if either speed effect holds out of sample, an in-game layer is built for the live simulator (the starter's probabilities moved by his measured speed against norm and his drift, as the game goes) and tested on in-game win chances before it goes live; if neither holds, the simulator's in-game updates keep pitch count as the only fatigue signal. Results (run 37876528060; 390,863 starters' plate appearances, 213,613 with drift defined, 279,204 with a start against norm): drift in fastball speed has SD 0.86 mph (mean -0.27), start against norm SD 1.01 mph. Run value per plate appearance: **drift -0.0068 runs per mph [-0.0090, -0.0046]** (a starter who has lost 1 mph since his first ten fastballs allows 0.0068 runs more per batter), spin -0.0018 per 100 rpm [-0.0035, -0.0002], height and extension nothing; **start against norm -0.0036 per mph [-0.0052, -0.0020]** (a start that opens 1 mph under his usual speed costs about 0.09 runs over 25 batters). Out of sample: start against norm +0.29 nats per 1,000 plate appearances [0.16, 0.43] in 2025 and +0.42 [0.24, 0.60] in 2026 through July; drift -0.05 and -0.12 (no gain: its effect is real but small next to pitch count, which already carries the average decline). **The third-time-through penalty is not velocity fatigue**: +0.0528 runs per plate appearance without drift, +0.0520 with it (drift accounts for 1.4%; predicted at least 15%). Predictions held for drift's size; start against norm is smaller than predicted (0.0036 against 0.005 to 0.020) and its 2025 gain (0.294) misses the 0.3 threshold by 0.006 while its interval excludes zero in both test sets. Under the rule (an effect that holds out of sample) the in-game layer is built for start against norm, after the matchup work; drift stays out. Reading: together with the Decision Horizon result that hitters do not read late movement better on later looks, the times-through-the-order penalty is neither lost velocity nor better reading of the pitch's flight; what remains is familiarity with the arsenal and sequence (SEQ-01 tests the expectation side) or fatigue that does not show in speed (command). | drift -0.0068 runs/PA/mph; TTO not explained by velocity |

Size check before building: the effect is -0.0036 runs per plate appearance per mph and start against norm has SD 1.0 mph, so once it is known (about ten fastballs in) it moves a starter's remaining expected runs by about 0.05 per SD and the game's win chance by about half a point; the Brier gain of a signal that size is about 0.00003, against LIVE-05's in-game interval of about ±0.0003. | **Deferred (product call not taken):** the registered in-game test cannot detect an effect a tenth of its noise, so the layer could only go live as an untested product call; it stays queued behind work that can be tested. The plate-appearance-level effect stands as measured. | deferred: below the in-game test's floor |
| COMMAND-01 | What changes inside a start before the box score shows it, beyond velocity (FATIGUE-01): the starter's location in his first 20 pitches against his own norm. Checked on synthetic games first: a planted command-of-the-day effect gains +1.04 nats per 1,000 plate appearances [0.69, 1.42] and gives a waste coefficient of +0.0070 runs per 10 points [0.0048, 0.0087]; with none, +0.04 [-0.06, +0.12] and +0.0010 [-0.0013, +0.0030] | **Registered in the commit that added this row, before the run.** Predictions: (1) the waste-rate deviation costs runs later in the start (positive per 10 points, interval excluding zero); (2) the command features improve 2025 out-of-sample log loss by at least 0.3 nats per 1,000 plate appearances with the interval excluding zero.
